=== NOTES 1:10 | Ivc Society and Polity – Stratification, Burials, Absence of Temples and Palaces
## SUB 0 | Burials – Types and Sites
[10:0:0] - **Common burial system (HPPSC 2012):** **extended earth burial with the head generally to the north**, and **grave goods** (pots, ornaments, copper mirrors) buried with the dead → "both a and b". Sitting-posture burial is not Harappan.
[10:0:1] - **Three forms:** complete (extended) burials, fractional burials, post-cremation burials.
[10:0:2] TABLE head: Burial practice | Site
  [10:0:2.0] Extended burials with goods; coffin burial | R-37, Harappa (head generally to the north)
  [10:0:2.1] Post-cremation / fractional, Late Harappan | Cemetery H, Harappa
  [10:0:2.2] Fractional (bones buried after exposure of the body) | Mohenjodaro, Harappa
  [10:0:2.3] Joint / double burial | Lothal (UPPSC 2016)
  [10:0:2.4] Dog buried with human, oval pit burials | Ropar
  [10:0:2.5] Pot burials; megalith-type grave with a big rock | Surkotada
  [10:0:2.6] Large cemetery of human burials (125 graves, N–S) | Sanauli (Sinauli), Baghpat, UP (UP Lower 2004)
  [10:0:2.7] Double burials; Mound 7 cemetery; women with jasper, agate beads, shell bangles | Rakhigarhi
[10:0:3] - **Kalibangan – three types:** extended burials in rectangular pits; pot burials; circular pits with grave goods but **no skeleton**.
[10:0:4] - Grave goods → belief in an **afterlife**; a copper mirror with cotton thread was found in a Harappa grave. Overall the Harappans did not bury precious things.
[10:0:5] - **Rakhigarhi Mound 7:** 56 skeletons incl. a ~4,600-year-old woman.
[10:0:6] - **Huddled skeletons** at Mohenjodaro (16 with ornaments) – once read as a "massacre", now as **multiple burials**.
[10:0:7] > Double burial = Lothal; dog with human = Ropar; pot burial = Surkotada; R-37 = extended; Cemetery H = Late Harappan.
[10:0:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.10, p.14-15; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.95, p.100; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.18, p.25; old app notes row 10
## SUB 1 | Social Stratification – Evidence
[10:1:0] TABLE head: Evidence | What it shows
  [10:1:0.0] Town layout | Citadel (west) – elite and public buildings vs lower town (east) – commoners
  [10:1:0.1] Houses | One to twelve rooms; single-roomed barracks at Harappa
  [10:1:0.2] Burial goods | Variation in goods and ornament quality = best sign of social differentiation
[10:1:1] - **Uniform bricks** prove organisation and authority, **not status**.
[10:1:2] - **No proof** of a rigid caste system; caste became fixed in the **Later Vedic** period. On the whole the Harappans seem relatively **egalitarian**.
[10:1:3] - Ranks and polity are rebuilt only from **material remains**; the script gives no rulers' names.
[10:1:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.100; old app notes row 10
## SUB 2 | Polity and Authority
[10:2:0] - Uniform pottery, seals, **weights**, **1:2:4 bricks** and **grid towns** over ~**8,00,000 sq km** show complex decisions made and carried out → a significant authority.
[10:2:1] TABLE head: View | Based on
  [10:2:1.0] Priestly rule | Citadels, Great Bath, steatite "Priest-King" (not conclusive – no temples)
  [10:2:1.1] Merchant rule | Trade-driven uniformity, few weapons
  [10:2:1.2] Several rulers / city-states | Several large cities, no single capital
  [10:2:1.3] Single state | Most plausible per PMF
[10:2:2] - Political structure **not clear**; it **seems corporate**; municipal-type civic administration (NextGen). Later Vedic = hereditary monarchy, weakening Sabha and Samiti.
[10:2:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.101; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.7; old app notes row 10
## SUB 3 | No Palaces, No Temples
[10:3:0] - **No royal palaces or temples** found. "Possessed great palaces and temples" = **WRONG** (UPSC 2013).
[10:3:1] - Worship was probably **domestic or open-air** (trees, animals); power **not personalised** in a king.
[10:3:2] - Public buildings were mainly **civic**: Great Bath, granary, pillared hall.
[10:3:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.101; old app notes row 10
## SUB 4 | Dress, Ornaments, Pastimes and Weapons
[10:4:0] - **Cotton and wool**; two garments (upper and lower); **Priest-King wears a shawl** with trefoil pattern; **no proof of stitching**.
[10:4:1] - **Ornaments:** gold, silver, ivory, copper, lapis lazuli; Allahdino gold and silver necklaces.
[10:4:2] - **Lipstick** = **Chanhudaro**; also bronze mirrors, ivory combs, collyrium sticks.
[10:4:3] - **Toy factory** = Chanhudaro; terracotta carts, animals, marbles, rattles, whistles (Karanpura); **dice** at Harappa and Mohenjodaro; board games (Lothal chess-like set).
[10:4:4] - **Weapons:** maces, axes, spears, bows and arrows of copper, bronze and stone; few and simple → relatively **peaceful**; **no horse-drawn chariots** (UPSC 2013).
[10:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.10-11; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.97, p.102; old app notes row 10

  x: Trade-driven uniformity, together with the absence of identifiable kings and palaces, has been used to argue for merchant elites or civic administrators.

=== NOTES 1:11 | Ivc Art and Craft – Dancing Girl, Priest-king, Terracotta, Beads, Pottery
## SUB 0 | Sculpture – Dancing Girl, Priest-King, Torso
[11:0:0] TABLE head: Sculpture | Site | Material
  [11:0:0.0] Dancing Girl (~10.5 cm / 4 inches; found 1926; National Museum, New Delhi) | Mohenjodaro | Bronze (lost-wax) – not clay, gold or carnelian (UPSC 2025)
  [11:0:0.1] Priest-King (bearded man in trefoil shawl, half-closed eyes) | Mohenjodaro | Steatite
  [11:0:0.2] Male torso | Harappa | Red sandstone / jasper
  [11:0:0.3] Daimabad man (chariot) | Daimabad | Bronze
[11:0:1] - The IVC gives the **first cities**, the **first script** (right to left) and the **earliest stone sculpture** (three-dimensional) in the subcontinent – but **not iron** sculpture; iron was unknown (CDS 2017).
[11:0:2] - Lifelike bronze and stone figures → **advanced metallurgy** and **craft specialisation**.
[11:0:3] - **18 Jun 2026:** **NCERT** to restore the original Dancing Girl image in the new **Class 9 Art** textbook.
[11:0:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.97-99, p.112 (CDS 2017); NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.8; old app notes row 11
[11:0:5] - **Dancing Girl excavated by Ernest Mackay** (1926, Mohenjodaro) – not R.D. Banerji (who found the site, 1922), Daya Ram Sahni or Wheeler.
## SUB 1 | Pottery
[11:1:0] - **Wheel-made**, sturdy, glossy; typical **red pottery** (red slip) **with black painted designs**; **plain red** ware the most common → colour = **red** (BPSC 1995).
[11:1:1] - Motifs: **geometric, floral, pipal leaf, fish-scale**, animals.
[11:1:2] - **Perforated jars**: large hole at the base, small holes in the wall – probably for **straining liquor**.
[11:1:3] - **Kalibangan** = six types of pottery; **Lothal** = painted jar (cunning fox story); big storage jars reached Oman.
[11:1:4] - Only evidence of painting is on pottery (no wall paintings).
[11:1:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.99; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.8, p.12; old app notes row 11
## SUB 2 | Lost-wax (Cire Perdue) and Metal Crafts
[11:2:0] - 1. Wax model coated with clay. 2. Heated: wax melts out. 3. Molten metal poured into the hollow mould. 4. Clay removed.
[11:2:1] - Lost-wax objects: **Dancing Girl**, **copper dog and bird of Lothal**, **bronze bull of Kalibangan**, buffalo and goat figures; continued in Late Harappan and Chalcolithic cultures (Daimabad bronzes) and later in **Chola** bronzes such as the **Nataraja**.
[11:2:2] TABLE head: Object | Site | Metal
  [11:2:2.0] Dog chasing a deer | Harappa | Bronze
  [11:2:2.1] Dog and bird | Lothal | Copper
  [11:2:2.2] Bull / ox | Kalibangan | Bronze
  [11:2:2.3] Chariot group, buffalo, rhinoceros, elephant | Daimabad | Bronze / copper
  [11:2:2.4] Gold foundry, active furnace | Rakhigarhi | Gold
  [11:2:2.5] Gold and silver necklaces | Allahdino | Gold, silver
[11:2:3] - Tools: chisels, needles, fishhooks, razors, mirrors, axes; vessels of copper, bronze, **silver**.
[11:2:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.72, p.88, p.97; old app notes row 11
## SUB 3 | Terracotta Figurines
[11:3:0] TABLE head: Type | Examples
  [11:3:0.0] Mother Goddess | Heavily jewelled female figurines (Harappa) – the most important terracottas
  [11:3:0.1] Toys | Toy carts with seated drivers (Chanhudaro), whistles, rattles, birds, gamesmen
  [11:3:0.2] Animals | Bulls (humpless bull prominent), birds, dogs, monkeys
  [11:3:0.3] Horned god | Clay mask of a horned god (Gujarat, Kalibangan)
[11:3:1] - Terracottas are crude, used by common people as toys or cult objects; seals and metal images were for the upper class.
[11:3:2] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.97-99; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.7; old app notes row 11
## SUB 4 | Beads, Faience and Shell
[11:4:0] TABLE head: Centre | Evidence
  [11:4:0.0] Chanhudaro | Bead-making factory, furnace, shell-working, bangles (APPSC G1 2022); lipsticks, ink pot; only city without a citadel
  [11:4:0.1] Lothal | Bead workshops; carnelian beads for export
  [11:4:0.2] Rakhigarhi | Gemstone factory – 3,000+ semi-precious stones
[11:4:1] - Materials: **carnelian** (from **Saurashtra**/Bharuch), agate, jasper, **steatite**, lapis lazuli, **faience**, **gold**; beads exported to **Mesopotamia** and the **Persian Gulf**.
[11:4:2] - **Faience** (ground sand/silica, glazed blue-green): beads, bangles, tiny vessels – called the **world's first glazed ware**; tie with **mosaic glass** = early clue to glass-making.
[11:4:3] - Shell work: Chanhudaro shell-ornament factory; Nageshwar, Balakot, Lothal.
[11:4:4] - Seal carving (steatite; unicorn commonest motif) and carnelian working = new techniques of the period.
[11:4:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.88, p.100; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.7-8, p.13; old app notes row 11

=== NOTES 1:12 | Ivc Religion – Pashupati Seal, Mother Goddess, Tree and Animal Worship, Fire Altars
## SUB 0 | Mother Goddess, Pashupati and Male Deities
[12:0:0] # Mother Goddess – the chief female deity
[12:0:1] - **Mother Goddess (Devi Mata):** terracotta female figurines, heavily jewelled, with fan-shaped head-dresses → **fertility cult**; probably the chief female deity (RAS 1993; SSC 2012: Devi Mata = **Indus Valley Civilisation**).
[12:0:2] - **Sites:** Harappa, Mohenjodaro, Kot Diji, Kalibangan, Banawali (with toy plough) and Gujarat sites; not a feature of Ropar (known for the dog burial).
[12:0:3] - **Continuity:** the Mother Goddess cult survives in later **Shakti cults**.
[12:0:4] - **⚠ Trap:** "Spirit and Brahma", "ritual" and "sacrificial system" are Vedic ideas – the Indus answer is **Mother Goddess**.
[12:0:5] # Pashupati seal – the chief male deity
[12:0:6] TABLE head: Feature | Detail
  [12:0:6.0] Site | Mohenjodaro (steatite seal)
  [12:0:6.1] Figure | Three-faced, horned head-dress, seated in a yogic posture
  [12:0:6.2] Animals around | Elephant, tiger, rhinoceros, buffalo; two deer below the seat – no lion
  [12:0:6.3] Identification | Proto-Shiva / Pashupati ("lord of animals") – John Marshall
  [12:0:6.4] Caution | Script undeciphered, so the reading is an inference from later Shiva worship
[12:0:7] - **Indus people worshipped Shiva** (proto-Shiva, UKPSC 2006) – Indra and Varuna are Rig Vedic gods; Brahma and Vishnu are later.
[12:0:8] - **Both male and female deities** were worshipped (Mother Goddess + Pashupati) – the only correct statement in UPSC 2013 (no palaces or temples, no horse-drawn chariots) → **"only 2"**.
[12:0:9] # Idol worship
[12:0:10] - **Idol worship began in the pre-Aryan (Harappan) period:** no temple has been found, yet idolatry was practised through figurines and seals (UPPCS 1992). The Rig Vedic Aryans had **no idols or temples**.
[12:0:11] > Exam angle: "people of the Indus Valley believed in …" → Mother Goddess; "beginning of idol worship" → pre-Aryan; "Shiva / Rudra" → Pashupati.
[12:0:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.7; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.99-101; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.108-109; app notes 1:12 (sub 0, 1)
## SUB 1 | Tree, Animal and Bird Worship; Fertility Symbols
[12:1:0] # Sacred trees, animals and birds
[12:1:1] - **Pipal:** the tree most often depicted on Indus seals (a deity standing among pipal branches) – MPPSC 2021; the pipal motif goes back to the **Early Harappan** phase and is still worshipped.
[12:1:2] - **Pigeon (dove):** listed by NextGen among sacred symbols – the bird "worshipped" by the Harappans (APPSC Group-II 2011); the peacock is only a pottery motif.
[12:1:3] - **Sacred animals:** the **unicorn** (mythical one-horned animal, the commonest seal motif), the **humped bull** (next in importance), elephant, tiger, buffalo, rhinoceros; cobras and horned deities also appear.
[12:1:4] - **Animals round Pashupati** were probably worshipped too.
[12:1:5] # Fertility and phallic symbols
[12:1:6] - **Linga and yoni:** conical stones (lingas) and **round pierced stones** (yoni) – fertility objects, possibly early Shaivite elements; found at Harappa.
[12:1:7] - **Swastika:** a seal motif, later an auspicious Hindu symbol – it has nothing to do with the Vedic yajna.
[12:1:8] - **Seals:** mostly of **steatite**, mostly **square** – some with a hole used as **amulets**.
[12:1:9] > Exam angle: "shape of which tree on Indus seals" → **pipal**; "bird worshipped" → **pigeon**; "mythical sacred animal" → **unicorn**.
[12:1:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.2, p.7; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.100; app notes 1:12 (sub 2)
## SUB 2 | Fire Altars, Great Bath and Ritual Practices
[12:2:0] # Fire altars
[12:2:1] TABLE head: Site | Evidence
  [12:2:1.0] Kalibangan (Rajasthan) | A row of seven fire altars on a mud-brick platform; triangular terracotta cake with a horned deity on one side and a human pulling a goat by a rope (animal sacrifice) on the other
  [12:2:1.1] Lothal (Gujarat) | Fire altar
  [12:2:1.2] Banawali, Rakhigarhi | Small pits with terracotta cakes regarded as fire altars
[12:2:2] - **Horned deity on a terracotta cake** → **Kalibangan** (State PSC) – not Banawali, Lothal or Surkotada.
[12:2:3] - **⚠ Caution:** Harappan fire altars are **not conclusive proof of the later Vedic yajna**.
[12:2:4] # Other ritual evidence
[12:2:5] - **Great Bath (Mohenjodaro):** probably for **ritual purification** (bathing).
[12:2:6] - **Amulets:** holed seals and amulets suggest belief in **ghosts and evil forces**; deities shown in **yogic poses** → practice of yoga and meditation.
[12:2:7] - **Burials with grave goods** point to a belief in life after death (details: burial row).
[12:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.7, p.12-13; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.101; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.20-21; app notes 1:12 (sub 1, 3)
## SUB 3 | Nature of Harappan Religion and Continuities
[12:3:0] - **Character:** predominantly **secular** civilisation – religion present but not dominant; nature- and **fertility-oriented** worship.
[12:3:1] - **No temple** has been identified anywhere; worship was domestic or open-air (under trees).
[12:3:2] - **Undeciphered script:** every reading of Harappan religion (proto-Shiva, Mother Goddess) is inferred from later traditions.
[12:3:3] - **Continuities into Hinduism:** Shiva/Pashupati, Shakti (Mother Goddess), linga-yoni, pipal and animal veneration, yoga.
[12:3:4] - **Contrast with the Rig Veda:** Vedic religion = **male nature gods and open-air yajnas**, no idols; Harappan = Mother Goddess, idols, animal and tree worship.
[12:3:5] - **Significance:** the earliest material evidence of a Shiva-like deity, a Mother Goddess and fertility cults in India.
[12:3:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.100-101; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.7, p.28; app notes 1:12 (sub 0, 5)

  What is shown on the other side of the horned-deity terracotta cake from Kalibangan?
  A row of fire altars | *A human pulling a goat by a rope | A unicorn before an incense stand | A woman with a pipal tree
  x: The triangular terracotta cake from Kalibangan shows a horned deity on one side and a human pulling a goat by a rope on the other.

  Where has a row of seven fire altars been found?

  x: Kalibangan yielded a row of seven fire altars. Fire altars have also been found at Lothal.

  x: Fire altars are known from Kalibangan (a row of seven) and also Lothal.

  x: Amulets show deities in yogic poses.

  x: Harappan religion was nature- and fertility-oriented; Later Vedic religion was sacrificial and ritualistic.
[nbc07a5f59b3] sub3
  Which of the following best expresses the historical significance of Harappan religious evidence?
  It demonstrates that Harappan religion was exclusively based on Vedic sacrifice. | *It provides early material evidence of cults involving a Shiva-like deity, a mother goddess and linga-related symbols that later show continuities. | It proves that the later Hindu religious system already existed in its complete form. | It establishes that all Harappan symbols had one fixed and universally accepted meaning.
  x: Harappan artefacts provide early material evidence of cultic elements that may have continued into later Hinduism. They do not prove that later Hinduism existed in a complete form or that every symbol has a certain interpretation.

=== NOTES 1:13 | Decline of the Ivc – Competing Theories; Late Harappan Cultures
## SUB 0 | Late Harappan Phase – Features and Sites
[13:0:0] # Features (c. 1900–1300 BCE, post-urban phase)
[13:0:1] - **Chalcolithic:** tools of **stone and copper**; **no metal objects requiring complicated casting**; people lived in **villages** (agriculture, stock-raising, hunting, fishing).
[13:0:2] - **Lost:** the extraordinary **uniformity** of the Mature phase (pottery, seals, bricks, weights), writing, long-distance trade, large public buildings and craft specialisation; pottery became **regionally varied**.
[13:0:3] - **Bhagwanpura (Kurukshetra, Haryana):** Late Harappan houses of **burnt brick** (so "no burnt bricks at Bhagwanpura" is **false**); Late Harappan pottery overlaps with **PGW**; a 13-roomed house (excavated by **J.P. Joshi**).
[13:0:4] - **APPSC Group-1 statements:** I chalcolithic ✓, II no complicated casting ✓, III villages ✓, IV no burnt bricks at Bhagwanpura ✗ → **I, II and III**.
[13:0:5] - **Astt. Comm. 2018:** uniformity of artefacts ✓ (Mature phase); "Late sites kept the same construction techniques" ✗ → **only I**.
[13:0:6] - **Iron** was absent in the Mature phase and came only later (PGW, Later Vedic).
[13:0:7] # Regional Late Harappan cultures
[13:0:8] TABLE head: Culture / site | Region
  [13:0:8.0] Cemetery H | Harappa region, Punjab (Pakistan)
  [13:0:8.1] Jhukar, Jhangar | Sindh
  [13:0:8.2] Rangpur | Kutch, Saurashtra and mainland Gujarat
  [13:0:8.3] Late Siswal, Bhagwanpura | Haryana
  [13:0:8.4] Bara, Dadheri | Punjab (India)
  [13:0:8.5] Alamgirpur | Uttar Pradesh (Meerut)
  [13:0:8.6] Daimabad | Maharashtra – southernmost Late Harappan site
  [13:0:8.7] Swat | Khyber Pakhtunkhwa
[13:0:9] - **Movement:** settlements shifted **eastward** to the Ganga–Yamuna Doab and **southward** to Gujarat and the Deccan.
[13:0:10] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.101, p.105, p.107; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.8; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.2, p.28; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.25; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagwanpura,_Haryana; app notes 1:13 (sub 3, 4)
## SUB 1 | OCP, Copper Hoards and Harappan–Vedic Continuity
[13:1:0] - **Ochre Coloured Pottery (OCP):** the Ganga–Yamuna Doab Late Harappan phase; **Copper Hoards** often found with OCP in the **Doab**.
[13:1:1] - **Continuity view:** identification of the Ghaggar-Hakra with the Rigvedic **Sarasvati**; Bhagwanpura's **Late Harappan + PGW** overlap.
[13:1:2] - **Traits that continued:** crafts, pottery, beads, weights, agriculture – **not iron tools**, which are post-Harappan.
[13:1:3] - **Rakhigarhi:** the only Harappan site so far to yield **ancient DNA**.
[13:1:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.8; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.28; app notes 1:13 (sub 5)
[13:1:5] - **Did not continue:** the **Harappan script** disappeared with the cities; wheat and cotton cultivation and proto-Shiva (Pashupati) worship carried on.
## SUB 2 | Decline Theories – Proponents
[13:2:0] TABLE head: Theory | Proponent(s)
  [13:2:0.0] Aryan invasion | Ramaprasad Chanda (1926), elaborated by Mortimer Wheeler (1947) – Indra "Purandara", huddled skeletons
  [13:2:0.1] Floods | Marshall, Mackay, S.R. Rao; floods + tectonic uplift – M.R. Sahni
  [13:2:0.2] Shift of the Indus course | H.T. Lambrick – Mohenjodaro abandoned
  [13:2:0.3] Climate change / aridity | Aurel Stein, A.N. Ghosh
  [13:2:0.4] Drying of the Ghaggar-Hakra (Sarasvati) | D.P. Agrawal and Sood – Kalibangan, Banawali abandoned
  [13:2:0.5] Ecological imbalance | W.A. Fairservis
  [13:2:0.6] Decline of trade with Mesopotamia (c. 1900 BCE) | trade-decline school
[13:2:1] - **Other stresses:** **soil salinity**, deforestation, malaria at **Mohenjodaro**; **Chanhudaro** was flooded several times.
[13:2:2] - **⚠ Trap:** Aurel Stein = climate, **not** Aryan invasion.
[13:2:3] - **Consensus:** decline was **gradual and multi-causal** – urban collapse over about **200 years** (c. 1900–1700 BCE) and a long tail to c. 1300 BCE; climate change and the drying of the Sarasvati are the most widely accepted causes.
[13:2:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.105-106; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.13; app notes 1:13 (sub 0)
## SUB 3 | Climate and the 4.2 ka Event
[13:3:0] - **4.2 ka event:** sharp weakening of the monsoon about **4,200 years ago**; the same aridity is linked to the fall of **Akkad** and Old Kingdom Egypt.
[13:3:1] - **Deoria Tal (Uttarakhand):** pollen record – the **oak-to-pine ratio** shift shows the monsoon **weakened sharply**.
[13:3:2] - **IIT Kharagpur study:** continuous low rainfall for about **900 years** drove the decline and migration.
[13:3:3] - **Ghaggar-Hakra:** the densest cluster of Harappan sites; its drying ended cities like Kalibangan.
[13:3:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.105; app notes 1:13 (sub 2)
## SUB 4 | Invasion Theory and Its Rejection
[13:4:0] - **Wheeler's case:** the Rig Veda's fort-breaking Indra (Purandara) and the huddled skeletons of Mohenjodaro.
[13:4:1] - **Rejected because:** the Rig Veda postdates the decline; the skeletons belong to different levels (multiple burials, not a massacre); there is no evidence of large-scale conflict between Late Harappans and Indo-Aryans.
[13:4:2] - **Rakhigarhi DNA (female, c. 4,600 years ago):** no **steppe pastoralist** component → challenges invasion theories, supports **indigenous continuity**; migration (not invasion) is still argued from other DNA studies.
[13:4:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.105-107; app notes 1:13 (sub 1)

=== NOTES 1:14 | Aryan Question – Original Home Theories and Archaeological Correlates
## SUB 0 | Meaning of 'Arya' – A Speech Group, Not a Race
[14:0:0] - **'Aryan' = a speech (linguistic) group:** speakers of Indo-Aryan, a branch of the **Indo-European** family – not an ethnic group, nomadic people or "superior race" (UPSC 1999, asked 3 times).
[14:0:1] - **'Arya' in classical Sanskrit:** a **noble man**; originally associated with "**high birth**" (UP Lower Sub 1998).
[14:0:2] - **Max Muller:** popularised the term in modern scholarship and was the first European to speak of an "**Aryan race**" (SSC 2006), though he later said it meant only a language group.
[14:0:3] - **William Jones** (18th c.) found the Sanskrit–Latin–Greek kinship that led to the idea of the Indo-European family.
[14:0:4] - **Languages in India:** Indo-European (Aryan) about **73%** of speakers (NextGen); **Dardic** branch – Jammu and Kashmir.
[14:0:5] > Exam angle: "The term Aryan denotes" → **a speech group**.
[14:0:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.18; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.120-122; NextGen Indian Geography APPSC Part 15 p.18; app notes 1:14 (sub 0)
## SUB 1 | Avesta and Boghazkoi – Linguistic and Epigraphic Evidence
[14:1:0] - **Avesta:** the **oldest Iranian** (Zoroastrian) text, in **Avestan, an old Iranian language**; closely similar to the Rig Veda → relates to **Iran** (UP Lower Sub 2004; APPSC FBO).
[14:1:1] TABLE head: Boghazkoi inscription | Fact
  [14:1:1.0] Place | Asia Minor (Turkey) – Hittite capital
  [14:1:1.1] Date | c. 1380 BCE (14th century BCE)
  [14:1:1.2] Content | Treaty between a Hittite king and a Mitanni king
  [14:1:1.3] Gods named | Indra, Mitra, Varuna, Nasatyas – not Agni
  [14:1:1.4] Significance | Vedic-speaking people or gods in West Asia; taken as evidence of Aryan movement via Iran
[14:1:2] - **⚠ Trap:** Mansehra and Shahbazgarhi are Ashokan rock edicts; Junagarh is Rudradaman's – none names Vedic gods.
[14:1:3] - **Other early Indo-European records:** Kassite (Iraq, c. 1600 BCE), Mitanni (Syria, 14th c. BCE).
[14:1:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.19, p.22; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.121; app notes 1:14 (sub 1)
## SUB 2 | Rigvedic Aryans vs Harappans
[14:2:0] TABLE head: Feature | Rigvedic Aryans | Harappans
  [14:2:0.0] Horse | Domesticated; horse-drawn chariots | No clear evidence
  [14:2:0.1] Warfare | Coat of mail (varman), helmet, bronze weapons | No such evidence
  [14:2:0.2] Metals | Gold, silver, copper (ayas) | Gold, silver, copper – not iron
  [14:2:0.3] Wheels | Spoked | Solid
  [14:2:0.4] Settlement | Rural, mud settlements | Planned urban cities
  [14:2:0.5] Transmission | Oral hymns | Seals and script
  [14:2:0.6] Dead | Cremation | Mostly burial
[14:2:1] - **UPSC 2017:** I (coat of mail) ✓, II ("Indus people knew copper and iron") ✗, III (horse) ✓ → **I and III only**.
[14:2:2] - **Early Vedic period:** c. **1500–1000 BCE**.
[14:2:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.119-121; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.19; app notes 1:14 (sub 5)
## SUB 3 | Original Home Theories
[14:3:0] TABLE head: Original home | Proponent
  [14:3:0.0] Central Asia | Max Muller
  [14:3:0.1] Arctic region | B.G. Tilak – *The Arctic Home in the Vedas* (1903)
  [14:3:0.2] Tibet | Dayananda Saraswati – *Satyarth Prakash*
  [14:3:0.3] Sapta Sindhu | A.C. Das – *Rigvedic India*; also Ganganath Jha (Brahmarshi-desha)
  [14:3:0.4] Hungary (Danube) | P. Giles
  [14:3:0.5] Southern Russia / Eurasian steppe | most plausible (NextGen); mainstream view
[14:3:1] - **⚠ Trap:** Arctic = Tilak, not A.C. Das.
[14:3:2] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.119; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.18; app notes 1:14 (sub 2)
## SUB 4 | Migration Debate – Archaeology and Genetics
[14:4:0] - **Mainstream:** Indo-Aryans came from Central Asia in waves, the Rig Vedic wave c. **1500 BCE**; migration, not invasion (supporters: **Asko Parpola**, Romila Thapar, Upinder Singh – migration with interaction and assimilation).
[14:4:1] - **Route:** **Andronovo** (steppe, horses, chariots) → **BMAC** (Turkmenistan, Uzbekistan, north Afghanistan – contact zone) → **Gandhara Grave culture** (Swat, horse remains, new burials): **3-1-2**.
[14:4:2] - **Indigenous / Out of India:** **S.R. Rao, B.B. Lal, Shrikant Talageri**; B.B. Lal linked **Painted Grey Ware** with the Vedic tradition (PGW–Later Vedic link still open).
[14:4:3] - **Genetics:** haplogroup **R1a**; 2021 *Science* paper – steppe migration 2000–1500 BCE.
[14:4:4] - **Rakhigarhi** (Hisar, Haryana; largest known Harappan site, on the seasonal **Drishadvati**): DNA of a woman of about 4,600 years ago with **no steppe ancestry**; **Mound 7** burial plot – **56** skeletons.
[14:4:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.106-107; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.22; app notes 1:14 (sub 3, 4)
## SUB 5 | Dasas, Dasyus and Panis
[14:5:0] - **Dasas:** a branch of the early Aryans (mentioned in Iranian literature); the Aryan chief was soft towards them.
[14:5:1] - **Dasyus:** the original inhabitants; possibly phallus-worshippers who kept no cattle for dairy; **Trasadasyu** = soft to Dasas, hostile to Dasyus.
[14:5:2] - **Divodasa** of the Bharata clan defeated **Sambara**.
[14:5:3] - **Panis:** non-Aryan cattle-holders and traders, condemned for greed.
[14:5:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.26; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.124; app notes 1:14 (sub 6)

=== NOTES 1:15 | Rig Vedic Period – Sapta Sindhu, Rivers, Tribes, Battle of Ten Kings
## SUB 0 | Rigvedic Rivers and Their Modern Names
[15:0:0] TABLE head: Rigvedic name | Modern river | Note
  [15:0:0.0] Sindhu | Indus | Most mentioned river (UPSC 1996); "Hiranyayi" (golden)
  [15:0:0.1] Sarasvati | Ghaggar-Hakra (or Avestan Haraxvati/Helmand) | Holiest: "Ambitame, Naditame, Devitame" – best of mothers, rivers, goddesses (Matetama = best of mothers)
  [15:0:0.2] Vitasta | Jhelum | –
  [15:0:0.3] Asikni | Chenab | –
  [15:0:0.4] Parushni | Ravi | Battle of Ten Kings
  [15:0:0.5] Vipas (Vipasha) | Beas | –
  [15:0:0.6] Shutudri | Sutlej | –
  [15:0:0.7] Drishadvati | Chautang | –
  [15:0:0.8] Kubha | Kabul | Afghanistan
  [15:0:0.9] Krumu | Kurram | Afghanistan
  [15:0:0.10] Gomati | Gomal | Afghanistan
  [15:0:0.11] Suvastu | Swat | Afghanistan / Pakistan
  [15:0:0.12] Sadanira (Later Vedic) | Gandak | Satapatha Brahmana
[15:0:1] - **Afghanistan link:** **Kubha, Krumu**, Gomati, Suvastu (UPPCS 1999, 2010) – Askini, Parushni, Vipasha, Shutudri are Punjab rivers.
[15:0:2] - **Yamuna** mentioned **thrice**, **Ganga once** (10th Mandala).
[15:0:3] - **⚠ Trap:** Parushni = Ravi, not Beas; Vipas = Beas.
[15:0:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.18, p.20; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.123-124; app notes 1:15 (sub 2), 1:16 (sub 6)
## SUB 1 | Battle of Ten Kings and the Tribes
[15:1:0] # Dasarajna (Battle of Ten Kings)
[15:1:1] - **Where:** banks of the **Parushni (Ravi)** (UPPCS 2008) – not the Vitasta, Vipasa or Asikni.
[15:1:2] - **Victor:** **Sudas**, Bharata king of the **Tritsu** family, with the priest **Vasishtha**.
[15:1:3] - **Opponents:** a confederacy of ten tribes – five Aryan (**Puru, Yadu, Turvasa, Anu, Druhyu**) and five non-Aryan – whose priest was **Vishvamitra**; rivalry of Vasishtha and Vishvamitra led to the war.
[15:1:4] - **Source:** **Mandala VII** of the Rigveda.
[15:1:5] - **After:** the defeated **Purus** joined the Bharatas → **Kurus**; the Kurus and Panchalas ruled the upper Ganga basin.
[15:1:6] - **NDA 2012 "not true":** "Purus sided with the Bharatas" ✗ – they were in the confederacy. The Rig Veda does mention Dasarajna and Sudas; it has about 300 non-Indo-European words.
[15:1:7] # Tribes
[15:1:8] - **Panchajana:** **Anu, Druhyu, Yadu, Turvasa, Puru** – **Kikata** is not one (UPPCS 2009, twice); the Kikatas lived in **south Bihar (Magadha)**.
[15:1:9] - **Other tribes:** Bharatas, Tritsus, Krivis, Srinjayas; **Bharatavarsha** is named after the **Bharatas**.
[15:1:10] - **Divodasa** (Bharata) defeated **Sambara**.
[15:1:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.26-27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.124-125; app notes 1:15 (sub 4, 5)
## SUB 2 | Sapta Sindhu, Chronology and Geography
[15:2:0] - **Period:** Early Vedic c. **1500–1000 BCE**; oldest core = **family books, Mandalas II–VII**.
[15:2:1] - **Sapta Sindhu:** land of seven rivers – Indus, its five Punjab tributaries and the Sarasvati; spread over **eastern Afghanistan, NWFP (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa), Punjab and Haryana**; the Later Vedic core shifted east to the Ganga–Yamuna Doab.
[15:2:2] - **Ganga is not one of the Sapta Sindhu**; Vipas is.
[15:2:3] - **Gandhara Grave culture (Swat):** horse remains – linked to the early Indo-Aryans.
[15:2:4] - **Terms:** **Himavant** = Himalayas; **Mujavant** = soma mountain in the **Hindu Kush**; **Dhanva** = desert land; **samudra** = a collection of water (the **Satapatha Brahmana** names the eastern and western seas).
[15:2:5] - **Later regions:** **Brahmarshi Desh** = Ganga–Yamuna Doab and adjoining area; **Madhya Desh** = between the Himalayas and the **Vindhyas**.
[15:2:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.18, p.20, p.22, p.29; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.123; app notes 1:15 (sub 0, 1, 3)

=== NOTES 1:16 | Early Vedic Polity – Rajan, Sabha, Samiti, Vidatha, Gana; Tribal Chiefdom
## SUB 0 | Vedic Assemblies – Sabha, Samiti, Vidatha, Gana
[16:0:0] TABLE head: Assembly | Nature | Key facts
  [16:0:0.0] Sabha | Small, select council of elders/nobles; advised the Rajan and had judicial functions | Mentioned 8 times in the Rigveda; women members Sabhavati; "narishta" in the Atharvaveda
  [16:0:0.1] Samiti | General tribal assembly of the people | Tribal business, legislation, choice of the chief
  [16:0:0.2] Vidatha | Oldest assembly – religious, military and distributive | Most mentioned (122 times); first to disappear in Later Vedic times
  [16:0:0.3] Gana | Tribal group / troop | Also a military unit – not a judicial body or a priest
[16:0:1] - **Daughters of Prajapati:** the **Atharvaveda** calls the **Sabha and Samiti** the two daughters of Prajapati – where they appear as separate institutions (UPPCS 2009; MPPSC 2024).
[16:0:2] - **Judicial assembly:** **Sabha** (JPSC 2016).
[16:0:3] - **RAS 1994:** the Sabha = a select council (closest: "**council of ministers**"); the "assembly of all the people" is the **Samiti**.
[16:0:4] - **Women:** attended the **Sabha and Vidatha**; the Atharvaveda mentions women in the **Samiti**; in the Later Vedic age women were excluded and the Vidatha vanished, while the Sabha and Samiti came under chiefs and nobles.
[16:0:5] - **Democratic element:** traces of election of the chief by the Samiti.
[16:0:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.26, p.29; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.128, p.131-132; app notes 1:16 (sub 1)
## SUB 1 | Nature of the Polity – Rajan and Tribal Chiefdom
[16:1:0] - **Tribal polity:** kinship-based **tribal chiefdom** (APPSC SQT 2018) – no formal bureaucracy, **no fixed territory**, no standing army.
[16:1:1] - **Hierarchy:** **Jana** (tribe; 275 mentions) → **Vis** (clan, under **Vispati**) → **Grama** (under **Gramani**) → **Kula** (family, under **Kulapa**); **janapada** is absent from the Rigveda.
[16:1:2] - **Rajan:** called **Gopati** (lord of cattle) / gopajanasya; protector of the tribe and cattle, **not ruler of a territory**; queen = **Mahisi**.
[16:1:3] - **Succession:** **monarchy**; the post tended to be **hereditary** (with traces of choice by the Samiti) → "**descended (hereditary) monarchy**" (RAS 1993).
[16:1:4] - **Divinity:** kingship not divine – only the Puru king **Trasadasyu** is called **ardhadeva** (demi-god).
[16:1:5] - **Later Vedic contrast:** territorial kingship, **Rajasuya**, **ratnins** and regular taxes.
[16:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.26, p.28; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.127-128; app notes 1:16 (sub 0, 2)
## SUB 2 | Officials, Army, Warfare and Bali
[16:2:0] TABLE head: Official | Role
  [16:2:0.0] Purohita | Chief priest and adviser (Vasishtha, Vishvamitra)
  [16:2:0.1] Senani | Army commander
  [16:2:0.2] Vrajapati | Officer in charge of pasture land (SI 2023)
  [16:2:0.3] Gramani | Head of the village / fighting band
  [16:2:0.4] Kulapa | Head of the family
  [16:2:0.5] Spasa | Spy
  [16:2:0.6] Duta | Messenger
  [16:2:0.7] Jivagribha, Ugra | Police officials
  [16:2:0.8] Madhyamasi | Mediator
[16:2:1] - **Army:** temporary tribal militia (**Sena**) in units **Sardha, Vrata and Gana**; foot soldiers (patti) and chariot-warriors (rathins); **bronze** weapons, **no iron**.
[16:2:2] - **Gavishti** = "search for cows" – the word for war.
[16:2:3] - **Bali:** a **voluntary offering** to the Rajan, not a regular tax.
[16:2:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.26-27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.125, p.128; app notes 1:16 (sub 3, 4)

3. It was kinship-based.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 and 2 only | *2 and 3 only | 1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: The early Vedic tribal polity was kinship-based with no fixed territory and no formal bureaucracy.
[g2dc3d09c2ff2] sub1
  Who was the head of the Vis (clan) in the early Vedic polity?
  Gramani | Kulapa | *Vispati | Senani
  x: The Vis was headed by the Vispati, the Grama by the Gramani and the Kula by the Kulapa.
[n2874ca425c0] sub1
  Who was the exceptional early Vedic ruler described as 'ardhadeva'?
  The Vispati of the Panchalas | The Gramani of the Bharatas | *Trasadasyu of the Purus | The Senani of the Kurus
  x: Kingship was generally not considered divine, but the Puru king Trasadasyu was exceptionally described as ardhadeva, or demi-god.
[n9080223d028] sub1
  By which title was the early Vedic Rajan known?
  *Gopati | Ekarat | Virat | Samrat
  x: The Rajan was called Gopati or Gopajanasya, protector of the tribe and its cattle.
[n4636e32364e] sub1
  Which statement most accurately reflects the succession to the early Vedic chiefship?
  It was strictly hereditary without any role for tribal assemblies. | *It generally tended towards hereditary succession, but the Samiti could have a role in selection. | It was always elected annually by all adult inhabitants. | It was reserved exclusively for the chief priest.
  x: Monarchy was the usual form and succession often passed within the family, but the office was not strictly hereditary and the Samiti had a role in selection.
[f7ba67b435493] sub1
  What was the queen called in the early Vedic polity?
  Sabhavati | Rishika | Gopa | *Mahisi
  x: The queen was Mahisi; Sabhavati were women members of the Sabha.
[nb7ce871ffd7] sub2
  What was 'Bali' in the early Vedic period?
  A military unit of chariot warriors | A judicial assembly of elders | A compulsory land tax collected by appointed officials | *A voluntary offering rather than a regular tax
  x: Bali was a voluntary offering. The early Vedic polity lacked a formal system of regular taxation and tax collection.
[n38564989950] sub2
  Into which units was the temporary military force of the early Vedic polity organised?
  Jana, Vis and Kula | *Sardha, Vrata and Gana | Sabha, Samiti and Vidatha | Bhaga, Shulka and Bali
  x: Sena was a temporary militia organised into units such as Sardha, Vrata and Gana.
[nfffec6ee3f2] sub2
  Who was the Vrajapati in the early Vedic polity?
  Spy | *Officer in charge of pasture land | Army commander | Chief priest
  x: Vrajapati was the pasture officer; Spasa was the spy, Senani the commander and Purohita the chief priest.
[n78477805c69] sub2
  What was the head of the family called in the Rigvedic period?
  Ugra | Vrajapati | *Kulapa | Jivagribha
  x: The family head was the Kulapa or Kulapati; Vrajapati was the pasture officer and Ugra and Jivagribha police officials.
[fbf91eec4b15b] sub2
  Who was the Spasa in the early Vedic polity?
  Village head | *Spy | Army commander | Mediator
  x: Spasa was a spy; Gramani was village head, Senani army commander and Madhyamasi mediator.
[f78291f98b77e] sub2
  What was the early Vedic army commander called?
  Purohita | Gramani | Vrajapati | *Senani
  x: The Senani commanded the army; the Purohita was chief priest and the Vrajapati was officer of pasture land.
[fafd233ca743e] sub2
  Who were Jivagribha and Ugra in the early Vedic polity?
  *Police officials | Tax collectors | Priests | Village heads
  x: Jivagribha and Ugra were police officials; there was no tax collector in the early Vedic polity.
[g8ea3d316d68b] sub2
  Who was the Purohita in the early Vedic polity?
  Army commander | Village head | *Chief priest and adviser | Officer of pasture land
  x: The Purohita was the chief priest and adviser of the Rajan.
[gc2b5adf4a97f] sub2
  Who was the Gramani in the early Vedic polity?
  *Head of the village | Head of the family | Spy | Mediator
  x: The Gramani was the village head and military leader of the grama.
[g944797c4e93b] sub2
  Which official acted as a mediator in the early Vedic polity?
  Spasa | *Madhyamasi | Vrajapati | Ugra
  x: The Madhyamasi was the mediator; the Spasa was a spy and the Ugra a police official.
[n09866ffaf27] sub2
  In the context of early Vedic warfare, what does the term 'gavishti' mean?
  Search for water sources | Search for fertile land | *Search for cows | Search for horses
  x: ‘Gavishti’ literally refers to the search for cows and denotes cattle raids, which were a common form of warfare in the Rig Vedic period.
[ndd12c7521e3] sub2
  What was the early Vedic Sena?
  *Temporary militia of tribesmen | Royal bodyguard | Mercenary force | Permanent standing army
  x: The Sena was a temporary militia of able-bodied tribesmen; there was no permanent standing army.
[n41c594a2e77] sub2
  Of which material were early Vedic weapons made?
  Iron | *Bronze | Stone | Steel
  x: Early Vedic weapons were of bronze, not iron; warriors used a coat of mail and helmet.
[f103dd17ad88e] sub2
  Which of the following was NOT known to early Vedic warfare?
  Coat of mail and helmet | Spoked-wheel chariot | *Iron weapons | Horse
  x: Early Vedic weapons were of bronze, not iron; coat of mail, helmet, horse and spoked-wheel chariot were known.

=== NOTES 1:17 | Early Vedic Society and Economy – Pastoralism, Gavishti, Varna Origins, Position of Women
## SUB 0 | Crafts, Metals, Trade and Exchange
[17:0:0] TABLE head: Craftsman | Term
  [17:0:0.0] Carpenter (chariots, boats) | Takshan
  [17:0:0.1] Smith / metal worker | Karmara
  [17:0:0.2] Goldsmith | Hiranyakara
  [17:0:0.3] Potter | Kulala
  [17:0:0.4] Weaver | Vaya
  [17:0:0.5] Physician | Bhishak
  [17:0:0.6] Chariot-maker | Rathakara
[17:0:1] - **Ayas** = **copper or bronze**; iron = **krishna ayas** (Later Vedic) – so the **blacksmith** craft was not in use (SSC 2003).
[17:0:2] - **Nishka:** a **gold neck ornament** (nishka-griva) in the Rigveda; later a **gold coin** (UPSC 1993; UPPCS 2007).
[17:0:3] - **Exchange:** barter, **no coinage**; the cow was the unit of value.
[17:0:4] - **Panis:** the **traders**, rich in cattle, condemned for greed and for hiding cattle (MPPSC 2019).
[17:0:5] - **Stupa:** the word first occurs in the **Rigveda**, meaning a **tree stem** / raised heap (CGPSC 2015).
[17:0:6] - **Occupational mobility:** hymn of a poet whose father is a physician and mother a grinder of corn.
[17:0:7] - **Bali:** a voluntary offering by tribesmen to the rajan.
[17:0:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.33, p.126; app notes 1:17 (sub 2, 3)
## SUB 1 | Pastoral Economy – The Cow
[17:1:0] - **Economy:** **mainly pastoral**, agriculture secondary (mixed economy).
[17:1:1] - **Cow = most valuable property** (UPPCS 2015) and the **standard unit of value and exchange**; **Gau** named **176 times**.
[17:1:2] - **Aghanya** = "**not to be killed**" – the Rig Vedic term for the **cow** (UPPCS 2008; UDA 2010; UPPSC 2017).
[17:1:3] - **Cattle words:** **Gavishti** (war = search for cows), **Gomat** (rich man with many cattle), **Duhitri** (daughter = one who milks), **Godhuli** (dusk, when cattle return – time), **Gavyuti** (distance), **Goghna** (guest fed on cattle).
[17:1:4] - **Horse:** asva is mentioned 215 times – vital for war and movement.
[17:1:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.125; app notes 1:17 (sub 0)
## SUB 2 | Agriculture, Crops and Food
[17:2:0] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [17:2:0.0] Yava | Barley – the chief Rigvedic grain
  [17:2:0.1] Dhanya | Grain / cereals
  [17:2:0.2] Vrihi | Rice – not in the Rigveda (later texts)
  [17:2:0.3] Godhuma | Wheat (later texts)
  [17:2:0.4] Mudga | Mung bean
  [17:2:0.5] Ikshu | Sugarcane
  [17:2:0.6] Sira / Langala | Plough drawn by bulls / wooden ploughshare
  [17:2:0.7] Sita | Furrow
  [17:2:0.8] Datra | Sickle
  [17:2:0.9] Urvara | Fertile land
  [17:2:0.10] Kshetra | Cultivated field
  [17:2:0.11] Kulya | Canal
  [17:2:0.12] Avata | Well
[17:2:1] - **Rice, wheat and cotton are not Rigvedic crops** – barley (yava) is the staple crop.
[17:2:2] - **RAS 2021 match:** Vrihi–rice, Mudga–mung, Yava–millet (only grain left in List-II), Ikshu–sugarcane → **A-ii, B-iii, C-iv, D-i**.
[17:2:3] - **Staple food:** **milk and its products** (ghee, curd), with barley (SSC 2008).
[17:2:4] - **Chief livelihood (UPPSC 1993):** cattle-rearing with farming; of the options, **agriculture**, which became the main livelihood in the Later Vedic age.
[17:2:5] - **Later Vedic contrast:** agrarian economy; **bhaga = 1/6** of produce; bali, bhaga and shulka collected by the **Bhagadugha**.
[17:2:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.126-127; app notes 1:17 (sub 1)
## SUB 3 | Position of Women
[17:3:0] - **Status:** relatively good (better than later) within a patriarchal society – "**somewhat superior**" (APPSC Sociology 2002); it **declined** in the Later Vedic period.
[17:3:1] - **Rights:** education, **choice of husband**, attendance at **assemblies**, sacrifices **with the husband**; no child marriage; **monogamy**; **niyoga** (childless widow with the husband's brother) and widow remarriage; could own cattle.
[17:3:2] - **Not:** holding the **highest positions of authority** (CDS 2014 – the incorrect statement); no women priests.
[17:3:3] - **Women seers (brahmavadinis):** **Lopamudra** (wife of **Agastya**, who Aryanised **South India**), **Ghosha**, **Apala**, **Vishvavara** – Rig Vedic (UPSC 1995).
[17:3:4] - **⚠ Trap:** **Gargi** is Later Vedic – her dialogue with Yajnavalkya is in the **Brihadaranyaka Upanishad** (not Rigvedic).
[17:3:5] - **Brahmavadini:** a woman who studied and expounded Vedic knowledge.
[17:3:6] - **Sons preferred**, but daughters were educated.
[17:3:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.129-130; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.28, p.31; app notes 1:17 (sub 6)
## SUB 4 | Family, Social Units and Varna Origins
[17:4:0] - **Family:** **patriarchal, patrilineal, patrilocal**, joint (three generations) under the kulapa; **monogamy** usual.
[17:4:1] - **Units (ascending):** **Kula – Grama – Vis – Jana**; **Vaishya** derives from **Vis**.
[17:4:2] - **Varna:** the word first occurs in the **Rigveda** (UPPSC 2012) – **arya varna vs dasa varna** = colour / cultural-linguistic distinction.
[17:4:3] - **Purusha Sukta (Rigveda X.90, 10th Mandala – a late addition):** first reference to the **four-fold varna** division (APPSC G1 2003, G1 PYQ) – Brahmana from the **mouth**, Kshatriya from the **arms**, Vaishya from the thighs, Shudra from the feet.
[17:4:4] - **Shudra:** first appears in the **10th Mandala**; not in the family books.
[17:4:5] - **Gotra:** in the Rigveda = cowpen; as lineage first in the **Atharvaveda**; gotra exogamy not yet established.
[17:4:6] - **Slavery:** slave women given as gifts to **priests**.
[17:4:7] - **Change:** the shift from cattle-tribe to agrarian economy explains later territorial kingship and varna hierarchy.
[17:4:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.22, p.28-29; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.127, p.129; app notes 1:17 (sub 4, 5)
## SUB 5 | Dress, Drinks and Pastimes
[17:5:0] - **Garments:** **Nivi** (undergarment), **Paridhana** (garment), **Adhivasa** (outer wrap) (CDS 2005).
[17:5:1] - **Drinks:** **soma** (ritual drink) and **sura** (fermented, intoxicating drink).
[17:5:2] - **Pastimes:** chariot racing, dicing – the **gambler's hymn (X.34)**, music and dance.
[17:5:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 1 p.20; app notes 1:17 (sub 7)

  x: Dasa and Dasyu captives and slave women are mentioned, including slave women gifted to priests, though slavery was not the base of the economy.
[f6aa804c774bd] sub4
  Which of the following is the correct ascending order of early Vedic social units?
  *Kula – Grama – Vis – Jana | Kula – Vis – Grama – Jana | Grama – Kula – Vis – Jana | Kula – Grama – Jana – Vis
  x: From smallest to largest: Kula (family), Grama (village), Vis (clan), Jana (tribe).
[n2cbb6e815b4] sub4
  The change from a cattle-based tribal economy to a settled agrarian economy helps explain the later rise of which of the following?
  Nomadic confederacies and flexible kingship | *Territorial kingship and varna hierarchy | Maritime empires and coin-based trade | Temple-centred urban democracy
  x: The shift from a cattle-based tribal economy to an agrarian territorial society contributed to the emergence of territorial kingship and a more established varna hierarchy.
[n6ff5b14e46d] sub4
  The Vaishya group is described as having developed from which group?
  The Dasas | The Karmaras | *The Vis | The Rajanyas
  x: Vaishyas were described as emerging from vis, meaning the mass of the people or tribespeople.
[n374d56262a4] sub4
  The Purusha Sukta occurs in which Mandala of the Rigveda?
  Mandala I | Mandala VII | *Mandala X | Mandala IX
  x: The Purusha Sukta (Rigveda 10.90), a late addition, is in Mandala X and is the first statement of the four-fold varna.
[nf0e6a4a857a] sub4
  What did the distinction between Arya varna and Dasa varna in the early Vedic period mainly reflect?
  *Cultural and linguistic distinction between groups | Division based exclusively on wealth | Rigid occupational caste system | Religious division between two priestly orders
  x: Arya and Dasa varna reflected a cultural, linguistic and partly colour-based distinction between Vedic groups and conquered local groups; it was not yet a rigid caste system.
[nec168f76c06] sub4
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched with the body part mentioned in the Purusha Sukta?
  *Brahmana — mouth | Kshatriya — feet | Vaishya — arms | Shudra — thighs
  x: The Purusha Sukta describes Brahmana as originating from the mouth, Kshatriya from the arms, Vaishya from the thighs and Shudra from the feet.
[nd5d22d0bd75] sub4
  Consider the following statements about gotra:
1. Gotra exogamy was fully established in the Rigvedic period.
2. In the Rigveda, the word gotra means cow-pen.
Which of the above statements is/are correct?
  1 only | *2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: In the Rigveda gotra means cow-pen; it appears in the lineage sense in the Atharvaveda, and gotra exogamy is a Later Vedic development.
[fbeddc866b1ee] sub4
  The word 'Shudra' first appears in which Mandala of the Rigveda?
  1st | 7th | 9th | *10th
  x: The word Shudra first occurs in the 10th Mandala, which also has the Purusha Sukta.
[fea79d9adf588] sub4
  In which text does the term 'gotra' first appear in the sense of lineage?
  Rigveda | Samaveda | *Atharvaveda | Satapatha Brahmana
  x: Gotra as lineage first appears in the Atharvaveda; in the Rigveda the word means cow-pen.
[f0be92e5948b4] sub4
  According to the Purusha Sukta, from which part of the Purusha's body did the Kshatriya emerge?
  Mouth | Feet | Thighs | *Arms
  x: Brahmana from the mouth, Kshatriya from the arms, Vaishya from the thighs and Shudra from the feet.
[g9bee4f593874] sub4
  What does the Purusha Sukta of the Rigveda (X.90) first state?
  The doctrine of karma | The four ashramas | The idea of Rita | *The four-fold varna division
  x: The Purusha Sukta in Mandala X is the first statement of the four-fold varna: Brahmana, Kshatriya, Vaishya and Shudra.
[gec4817e383ce] sub4
  Consider the following statements:
1. The word varna first appears in the Rigveda.
2. The Purusha Sukta belongs to the oldest family books of the Rigveda.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: The word varna first occurs in the Rigveda, but the Purusha Sukta is in Mandala X, a late addition, not the family books (II–VII).
[f57096b14a98e] sub5
  Which is the gambler's hymn of the Rigveda?
  IX.1 | *X.34 | X.90 | VII.18
  x: The gambler's hymn on dicing is Rigveda X.34; X.90 is the Purusha Sukta.
[fb4de0b715784] sub5
  What was 'sura' in the Rig Vedic age?
  *Fermented drink | Sacred plant | Chariot race | Musical instrument
  x: Sura was a fermented drink with few Rigvedic references; soma was the sacred drink.

=== NOTES 1:18 | Later Vedic Period – Eastward Expansion, Iron (krishna Ayas), Painted Grey Ware
## SUB 0 | Eastward Expansion – Kuru-Panchala, Videgha Madhava, Agastya
[18:0:0] # Period and spread
[18:0:1] - **Later Vedic (post-Rigvedic) period = 1000–600 BC (BCE).** Texts composed in the upper Gangetic basin.
[18:0:2] - **Course:** **Punjab → Ganga-Yamuna Doab** (Kurukshetra, Delhi, upper Doab) → **Kosala** (eastern UP), **Kashi** (Varanasi), **Videha** (north Bihar), reached by about 600 BCE.
[18:0:3] - **Kurus** = union of the **Bharatas and Purus**; first between the Sarasvati and Drishadvati, then Delhi and the upper Doab (Kurukshetra). **Panchalas** = middle Doab (Bareilly, Badaun, Farrukhabad).
[18:0:4] - **Kuru-Panchala** = **centre / axis of Aryan culture** in the post-Vedic period: the Vedic texts were compiled here (not Anga-Magadha, Kosala-Videha or Matsya-Shurasena).
[18:0:5] TABLE head: Place | Capital of
  [18:0:5.0] Hastinapur (Meerut district) | Kuru kingdom (Kauravas)
  [18:0:5.1] Indraprastha (Purana Qila, Delhi) | Kuru kingdom (Pandavas)
  [18:0:5.2] Ahichchhatra (Ramnagar, Bareilly) | Panchala
[18:0:6] # Videgha Madhava legend (Shatapatha Brahmana)
[18:0:7] - **Videgha Madhava** (Mathava), king of Videha, lived on the **Sarasvati**; with his priest **Gautama Rahugana** he followed **Agni Vaishvanara** (sacrificial fire) as it burnt the forests eastward.
[18:0:8] - They crossed the **Sadanira (Gandak)**; the land beyond was settled as **Videha**.
[18:0:9] - Read as the eastward movement and agricultural colonisation by **burning and clearing forests** (fire and iron axes).
[18:0:10] # Aryanisation of the South
[18:0:11] - **Agastya** = the sage said to have **Aryanised South India** (crossed the Vindhyas); not Vishvamitra, Vasishtha or Apastamba.
[18:0:12] > Axis of Aryan culture = Kuru-Panchala; Videgha Madhava's priest = Gautama Rahugana; river crossed = Sadanira (Gandak).
[18:0:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.30; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.131, p.139, p.148; old app notes 1:18
[18:0:14] - **Hastinapura** (Meerut district, UP) lies about 100 km north-east of **Delhi** – of Lucknow, Patna, Delhi, Chandigarh the nearest modern city is Delhi.
## SUB 1 | Pottery Sequence and Painted Grey Ware
[18:1:0] # Sequence of protohistoric pottery (Ganga-Yamuna Doab)
[18:1:1] TABLE head: Order | Pottery | Date | Phase
  [18:1:1.0] 1 | Ochre Coloured Pottery (OCP) | 2000–1500 BCE | Chalcolithic
  [18:1:1.1] 2 | Black and Red Ware (BRW) | 1450–1200 BCE | Chalcolithic; also Later Vedic; Megalithic south
  [18:1:1.2] 3 | Painted Grey Ware (PGW) | 1200–500 BCE | Early Iron Age / Later Vedic
  [18:1:1.3] 4 | Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) | 700–100 BCE (from c. 600 BCE in the Ganga valley) | Mahajanapadas, second urbanisation
[18:1:2] - **OCP** is an ill-fired red ware that leaves an **ochre colour on the fingers**; **christened (named) at Hastinapur by B.B. Lal (1950–52)**, not Ahichchhatra, Noh or Red Fort.
[18:1:3] - **Four wares known to the Later Vedic people:** **Red Ware** (most popular), **Black-slipped Ware**, **Black-and-Red Ware**, **Painted Grey Ware** (most distinctive) = I, II, III, IV. **NBPW is NOT** Later Vedic (post-Vedic, shiny black).
[18:1:4] # Painted Grey Ware culture
[18:1:5] - **Grey pottery with black linear / geometric painted designs**; mainly **bowls and dishes**; deluxe tableware of the upper strata; not polished black.
[18:1:6] - **Pottery of 1000–600 BC in the Gangetic plain = PGW.**
[18:1:7] - About **700 sites**, mainly in the **upper Ganga basin** and the Indo-Gangetic divide (Punjab, Haryana, western UP) – not the lower Indus basin.
[18:1:8] - First found at **Ahichchhatra** (excavations 1940–44); **Hastinapur** excavation (1950–52, **B.B. Lal**) established it as a culture.
[18:1:9] - Finds: **iron weapons**, copper objects (copper possibly from **Khetri**, Rajasthan), glass, bone, semi-precious beads (trade, barter; no coins).
[18:1:10] TABLE head: Site | Region
  [18:1:10.0] Hastinapur | Meerut, western UP (Kuru capital)
  [18:1:10.1] Atranjikhera | Etah, western UP (Doab)
  [18:1:10.2] Ahichchhatra | Bareilly, UP
  [18:1:10.3] Bhagwanpura | Haryana – 13-roomed mud house; pre-iron PGW with late Harappan pottery
  [18:1:10.4] Noh | Rajasthan (OCP–BRW–PGW sequence)
[18:1:11] - Early PGW (Ghaggar-Hakra, Bhagwanpura) has **no iron**; later Doab PGW is iron-using.
[18:1:12] > Later Vedic wares exclude NBPW; PGW = upper Ganga basin, 1000–600 BC; OCP named at Hastinapur.
[18:1:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.30, p.32; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.133; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.6-9; old app notes 1:18
## SUB 2 | Iron – Krishna Ayas, Dates and Earliest Sites
[18:2:0] # Vedic metal terms
[18:2:1] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [18:2:1.0] Ayas (Rigveda) | Copper or bronze
  [18:2:1.1] Krishna ayas / Shyama ayas ("black metal") | Iron (Later Vedic texts; first in the Yajurveda)
  [18:2:1.2] Lohita ayas | Copper
  [18:2:1.3] Hiranya | Gold
  [18:2:1.4] Nishka | Gold ornament; later a unit of value
[18:2:2] # Iron in North India
[18:2:3] - Iron technology about **1000–800 BCE**; common at most **PGW sites** of the Doab; iron weapons (arrowheads, spearheads) common in western UP from about 800 BCE.
[18:2:4] - Iron axes and fire cleared Gangetic forests; knowledge of iron reached Kosala and Videha by the end of the period.
[18:2:5] - **Earliest iron among the classic northern sites = Atranjikhera** (Etah, UP; excavated by R.C. Gaur) – not Taxila, Kaushambi or Hastinapur. Other early sites: Hastinapur, Kaushambi, Taxila (Gandhara, c. 1000 BC).
[18:2:6] - **Jakhera** (UP): iron in the **Black and Red Ware phase, before 1200 BCE**; iron in Dharwar (Karnataka) c. 1000 BCE.
[18:2:7] # Earliest iron – Tamil Nadu dates (2025)
[18:2:8] TABLE head: Site | District (TN) | Finding
  [18:2:8.0] Sivagalai | Thoothukudi | Iron dated to about 2953 BCE (to 3345 BCE) – now the oldest (TN report, Jan 2025)
  [18:2:8.1] Mayiladumparai | Krishnagiri | Iron AMS-dated to 2172 BCE; earliest claim before Sivagalai
  [18:2:8.2] Thirumalapuram | Tenkasi | Iron Age urn chamber
  [18:2:8.3] Adichanallur | Thoothukudi | Urn burials; not a PGW or northern iron site
[18:2:9] - These dates push iron use far earlier than the Gangetic 1000 BCE; they say **nothing about where PGW began**.
[18:2:10] - **First metal used in India = copper** (Chalcolithic = "copper-stone" age, Greek *khalkos* + *lithos*); iron came later.
[18:2:11] > Krishna ayas = iron, not copper; Sivagalai (2953 BCE) is older than Mayiladumparai (2172 BCE).
[18:2:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.30, p.32; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.132; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.7; old app notes 1:18
## SUB 3 | Agriculture, Settlements and Crafts
[18:3:0] - **Agriculture** became the main livelihood; wooden ploughshare common; ploughs drawn by **6, 8, 12 and even 24 oxen**.
[18:3:1] TABLE head: Crop | Later Vedic term
  [18:3:1.0] Rice | Vrihi (ritual use; rice remains of 8th c. BCE at Hastinapur, Atranjikhera)
  [18:3:1.1] Wheat | Godhuma
  [18:3:1.2] Barley | Yava (continued)
  [18:3:1.3] Sugarcane | Ikshu
[18:3:2] - **Settlements:** mainly **villages** of mud-brick and wattle-and-daub houses; **burnt bricks not common**; Hastinapur and Kaushambi only proto-urban; **true towns** came in the **NBPW phase** (second urbanisation: Shravasti, Kausambi, Hastinapur).
[18:3:3] - **Crafts:** carpentry (chariots), leather, pottery, weaving (by women), copper-bronze-iron smiths; **Shresthins** = merchant groups; units of value **nishka, satamana, krishnala**; barter still main.
[18:3:4] - **AP angle:** Iron Age in Andhra = **Megalithic culture with BRW** (Brahmagiri, Nagarjunakonda, Gudivada, Jonnawada); H.D. Sankalia: rice first harvested in AP in the Iron Age (Megalithic) era.
[18:3:5] - **Sep 2026:** BHU opened 22 sealed boxes of 10,000+ **Rajghat** (Varanasi) artefacts; pre-NBPW pottery dates Varanasi's urban origins to about **800 BCE**.
[18:3:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.32; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.132-133; old app notes 1:18
## SUB 4 | Later Vedic Religion – Gods and Priests
[18:4:0] TABLE head: God | Position
  [18:4:0.0] Prajapati | Creator; became supreme
  [18:4:0.1] Vishnu | Rose as preserver (minor in the Rigveda)
  [18:4:0.2] Rudra | God of animals; later merged with Shiva
  [18:4:0.3] Pushan | Cattle god; god of the Shudras
  [18:4:0.4] Indra, Agni, Varuna | Lost importance
[18:4:1] - Prayers declined; elaborate **sacrifices** grew, raising the priests' power; Upanishadic thinkers criticised **ritualism**.
[18:4:2] TABLE head: Priest | Veda / role
  [18:4:2.0] Hotri | Recited the Rigveda
  [18:4:2.1] Adhvaryu | Yajurveda; did the practical, manual work of the sacrifice
  [18:4:2.2] Udgatri | Chanted the Samaveda
  [18:4:2.3] Brahman | Atharvaveda; supervised
[18:4:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.33; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.139; old app notes 1:18

=== OWN MCQs of 1:18 (58)
[n67834d39cb5] sub0
  Which one of the following represents the broad sequence of the Later Vedic eastward expansion?
  *Punjab → Ganga-Yamuna Doab → Kosala, Kashi and Videha | Videha → Punjab → Kuru-Panchala → Kosala | Kashi → Punjab → Videha → Kuru-Panchala | Kosala → Punjab → Kashi → Ganga-Yamuna Doab
  x: The movement proceeded from Punjab into the Ganga-Yamuna Doab, including Kurukshetra and the Delhi-upper Doab, and then towards Kosala, Kashi and Videha by about 600 BCE.

  x: The Shatapatha Brahmana narrates how Videgha Madhava and Gautama Rahugana followed Agni Vaishvanara eastward from the Sarasvati.
[n7ad063b2239] sub0

  x: The Kurus resulted from the union of the Bharatas and Purus; Kuru-Panchala, with its capital at Hastinapur, became the centre of Aryan culture.
[nd668fbe2dec] sub0

  Manufacturing Northern Black Polished Ware | *Clearing the forests of the Ganga region with iron axes and fire | Introducing bronze metallurgy into the Indus cities | Constructing stone megaliths in the far south
  x: The Videgha Madhava legend is connected with the clearing of Ganga-region forests through the use of iron axes and fire. It illustrates the role of iron in eastward expansion.

  x: Videgha Madhava and his priest Gautama Rahugana followed Agni Vaishvanara eastward from the Sarasvati.

  Kaushambi | Shravasti | Ahichchhatra | *Hastinapur

[g4c96da972452] sub0

  x: Tradition credits the sage Agastya with spreading Aryan culture to South India.
[n965502748b4] sub1
  Ochre Coloured Pottery is generally placed in which period?
  *Second millennium BCE | Third millennium BCE | First millennium BCE | First millennium CE
  x: Ochre Coloured Pottery is post-Harappan and belongs to the second millennium BCE; the hallmark Later Vedic pottery is Painted Grey Ware.
[nc16fae2d45e] sub1
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the pottery sequence of Indian protohistory?
  Ochre Coloured Pottery comes before Black and Red Ware | Black and Red Ware comes before Painted Grey Ware | *Painted Grey Ware comes after Northern Black Polished Ware | Northern Black Polished Ware begins around 600 BCE
  x: The sequence runs OCP, Black and Red Ware, Black Slipped Ware, Painted Grey Ware, Red Ware and then NBPW, so PGW comes before NBPW, not after it.
[n7b2926d47f4] sub1
  Which of the following groups contains the four wares known in the Later Vedic phase?
  Ochre Coloured Pottery, Black and Red Ware, Northern Black Polished Ware and Red Ware | Black Slipped Ware, Painted Grey Ware, Northern Black Polished Ware and Ochre Coloured Pottery | *Black and Red Ware, Black Slipped Ware, Painted Grey Ware and Red Ware | Painted Grey Ware, Northern Black Polished Ware, Red Ware and Amphora Ware
  x: The four Later Vedic wares are Black and Red Ware, Black Slipped Ware, Painted Grey Ware and Red Ware. Northern Black Polished Ware is associated with the later Mahajanapada and second-urbanisation phase, not the Later Vedic group.
[f3532a11b2f30] sub1

[fb4e20358deda] sub1

  Painted Grey Ware | Black Slipped Ware | Black and Red Ware | *Northern Black Polished Ware
  x: NBPW, from about 600 BCE, is the shiny black ware of the Mahajanapada phase; PGW is grey.
[gb2367d799843] sub1
  Which of the following types of pottery was NOT used by the Later Vedic people?
  Black Slipped Ware | Painted Grey Ware | Red Ware | *Northern Black Polished Ware
  x: NBPW began only around 600 BCE with the Mahajanapadas; Black Slipped Ware, Painted Grey Ware and Red Ware are Later Vedic wares.
[n8c35b4e13d1] sub1
  Consider the following statements about Painted Grey Ware:

2. It consists mainly of bowls and dishes.
3. It is a highly polished black pottery.
Which of the above statements are correct?
  1, 2 and 3 | 2 and 3 only | *1 and 2 only | 1 and 3 only
  x: PGW is grey pottery with linear painted designs, mostly bowls and dishes (c. 1000-600 BCE). Highly polished black pottery describes NBPW.
[nfebbd44c40a] sub1
  With reference to the Painted Grey Ware culture, which one of the following statements is NOT correct?
  The sites are concentrated mostly in the upper Ganga basin. | *The culture was confined exclusively to the lower Indus basin. | About 700 sites have been identified. | The sites yielded iron weapons and copper objects.
  x: Painted Grey Ware sites were found mainly in the upper Ganga basin, not exclusively in the lower Indus basin. They yielded iron weapons, earthen bowls and dishes, and copper objects.
[f6d8adc000737] sub1

  x: PGW was first found at Ahichchhatra (1944); the Hastinapur excavation (1950-52) established it as a culture.

  x: Bhagwanpura is in Haryana; Noh is in Rajasthan and Atranjikhera in western UP.

  x: About 700 PGW sites are known, mainly in the upper Ganga basin.

  In which region are the Painted Grey Ware sites mainly found?
  Lower Indus basin | *Upper Ganga basin | Narmada valley | Lower Ganga delta
  x: PGW sites lie mainly in the upper Ganga basin, not the lower Indus basin.
[gdafc6ab8feb1] sub1

  Early Vedic period, 1500-1000 BC | *Later Vedic period, 1000-600 BC | Harappan period, 2600-1900 BC | Mauryan period, 322-185 BC
  x: Painted Grey Ware is the pottery of the Later Vedic people in the upper Ganga basin, dated about 1000-600 BC.
[gd48490bc5f05] sub1
